Corrections made against the issue's own description: the payment challenge travels in the `WWW-Authenticate: Payment` header, not a `402` response body; the issue's `401`-vs-`403` "depending on protocol state" is shipped as its own hedge, not a spec-confirmed mapping, because no normative condition-to-status-code table was found; no protocol-defined pricing taxonomy exists for per-token/per-MB/dynamic pricing, so the skill points to the real registered `intent` values (`charge`, `session`, `subscription`) instead; and MPP's MCP integration is documented more precisely than the issue's example — a dedicated transport binding (`draft-payment-transport-mcp-00`) with JSON-RPC error code `-32042`. One reference URL in the issue (`https://www.machinepaymentsprotocol.org/`) does not resolve and is not cited anywhere in the skill. `references/protocol-sourcing.md` carries the full source table and label rationale so the SKILL.md body stays under the `skill-check.config.json` `maxBodyTokens`/`maxBodyLines` limits. Security guidance defers to, and does not duplicate, `rules/security/backend.md` and `@skills/laravel-security/SKILL.md` — it states only the MPP-specific additions (single-use payment proofs, request/body binding via HMAC and RFC 9530 digest, no side effects before payment, idempotent settlement under concurrency, TLS 1.2+ required), each cited at the draft's own conformance level (MUSTs, with request binding as a SHOULD). This is the 51st skill in the catalog; the two content-pin tests (`tests/Installer/SkillCatalogContentTest.php`, `tests/Installer/SkillsContentTest.php`) derive the count from the skills directory and pin `README.md`'s literals, so the README numbers cannot silently drift.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: `composer build` / `composer check` no longer fail on `rector/rector` 2.6.1 (#189). That release turns two rules registered by `vendor/pekral/rector-rules` into hard per-file system errors instead of no-ops, so `vendor/bin/rector process --dry-run` exited 1 on every processed file regardless of whether the file had anything to do with either rule. Both are now in `rector.php`'s existing skip list: `UnusedForeachValueToArrayKeysRector` (named in the issue) and `ArraySpreadInsteadOfArrayMergeRector` — **the second one is not in the issue text**; it is deprecated by the same release, fails identically, and skipping only the first still left the build red, verified by reproducing the failure locally on 2.6.1 before and after each skip. This is the third occurrence of one defect class (see the `>= 2.5.9` data-provider docblock entry and its comment in `rector.php`), and the mechanism guarantees more: `composer.lock` is gitignored, so CI resolves dependencies fresh on every run while a local checkout keeps whatever it installed earlier — which is exactly why CI went red on `b43e6aa` while the local build on the identical commit was green. The structural options (tracking `composer.lock`, constraining `rector/rector` to a tested minor, pinning `pekral/rector-rules` to a tag instead of `dev-master`) need a maintainer decision and are deliberately **not** taken here; this entry fixes the break, it does not close the recurrence.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: three security rule files, plus a PHP examples file, now carry Claude Code's actual, documented path-scoping key instead of a Cursor-only one that Claude Code silently ignores (#162). Iteration 1 of this fix added `alwaysApply: false` + `globs: [...]` to `rules/security/{backend,frontend,mobile}.md` and `rules/php/examples/named-arguments.md` — but `globs`/`alwaysApply` are **Cursor** frontmatter keys, not Claude Code ones. Per https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory ("Organize rules with `.claude/rules/`" → "Path-specific rules"), fetched and re-verified directly for this fix: "Rules can be scoped to specific files using YAML frontmatter with the `paths` field" and "Rules without a `paths` field are loaded unconditionally and apply to all files." Claude Code has no `globs`/`alwaysApply` field at all, so iteration 1 changed **0 bytes** of what Claude Code actually loads. This fix renames the key to **`paths:`**, using the documented block-sequence list form (`rules/laravel/filament.mdc` / `livewire.mdc` already use this exact form in this repo): `backend.md` → `app/**/*.php`, `src/**/*.php`, `packages/**/*.php`, `Modules/**/*.php`, `bootstrap/**/*.php`, `config/**/*.php`, `database/**/*.php`, `routes/**/*.php`, `tests/**/*.php` (the `src/**`/`packages/**`/`Modules/**` entries are new in this iteration — this repo's own `src/` tree would not have matched the iteration-1 pattern set, which only covered `app/**`); `frontend.md` → `resources/**/*.js`, `resources/**/*.ts`, `resources/**/*.jsx`, `resources/**/*.tsx`, `resources/**/*.vue`, `resources/**/*.blade.php`, `resources/**/*.css`, `resources/**/*.scss`, `public/**/*.js` (rendered as an explicit list per file, not the brace-expansion `{js,ts,...}` shorthand the file itself never used); `mobile.md` → `mobile/**`, `**/*.swift`, `**/*.kt`, `**/*.dart`. `rules/php/examples/named-arguments.md` cannot cleanly repeat the iteration-1 "reference-only, `globs: []`" intent: the docs define only two cases — absent `paths` (unconditional) and a `paths` list (loads when a matching file is touched) — and do **not** document what an empty `paths: []` list means, so guessing it means "never matches" would repeat the same category of mistake this fix corrects. Given that gap, `named-arguments.md` instead gets `paths: ["**/*.php"]` — a real, documented, verifiable scope (loads only when Claude works with a PHP file) that is broader than "only when explicitly referenced from `core-standards.mdc`" but is not a guess; its reference from `core-standards.mdc:82` is untouched. All four rule bodies stay byte-identical below the frontmatter — confirmed by SHA-256 digest, not just a heading prefix — and `tests/Installer/RuleFrontmatterScopingTest.php` / `tests/Installer/NamedArgumentsExamplesFrontmatterTest.php` pin the corrected key, the path lists, and the digests as a regression gate; the pre-existing `tests/Installer/SecurityContentTest.php` pins remain the durable protection against a rule-*wording* regression. Two of the issue's acceptance criteria were already satisfied and are recorded as such rather than re-implemented: root `CLAUDE.md` is 69 lines (well under the requested 200), and it contains zero `@import` / `@rules/` tokens, so "imports are not used as fake lazy-loading" had nothing to fix. The issue's "Expected Architecture" tree and its 5-phase directory-migration plan describe an ordinary consumer Laravel application's `.claude/` layout and are not applicable here: this repository is the distribution *package* (`src/InstallerPath.php`) that already ships that separation via its tracked `rules/`, `skills/`, and `docs/` directories, and `.claude/` in this checkout is the gitignored, installer-generated copy those directories produce, not a tracked source to edit directly. **What this fix does and does not prove:** the frontmatter key is now the one the docs define, and the docs state plainly that an absent `paths` field means unconditional loading — that much is fetched-and-quoted, not inferred. What is still **not** end-to-end confirmed in this non-interactive run is that a live Claude Code session actually stops loading these four files outside their `paths` scope; the docs also mention a non-interactive `InstructionsLoaded` hook for exactly this kind of verification, which was not run here either (see the PR body for why). This entry deliberately does **not** claim a specific byte or percentage reduction, and does not claim "no regression is possible" — *when* a rule loads is as load-bearing as *what it says*, so narrowing the trigger is a behavior change that stands or falls on the same verification the load-time claim itself needs, not a claim that can be waved through as metadata-only. A related, distinct defect surfaced independently while investigating this issue — every `.mdc` rule file (Cursor's format) appears to never reach Claude Code at all, including six files that declare `alwaysApply: true` and are therefore currently silently inert for Claude Code users — is filed separately as #187 rather than fixed here, since it needs a maintainer decision on Cursor-compatibility tradeoffs, not a blind rename.

- ✨ **Added**: least-privilege controls for the agent roster, closing four of the six sub-issues #160's security-risk analysis split out (#163, #165, #167, #168; #166 already merged via #182). This entry states plainly what is **enforced** by the harness today, what is an **obligation** every agent follows without being blocked by the harness, and what is **advisory** — a declared boundary the harness does not check — because the architecture constraint (`docs/agents.md` *Architecture constraint*) is that this package ships instructions only, never a runtime permission engine, logging daemon, or consent broker. **Enforced:** every agent's `tools:` allow-list (already pinned by `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php`) plus a new `disallowedTools:` frontmatter entry on every shipped agent — read-only agents (`athena`, `hermes`, `daedalus`) lose `Write, Edit`; agents with no documentation-fetch need (`talos`, `apollon`) lose `WebSearch, WebFetch` — a second, independent layer against `tools:` drift (#163). **Advisory (not harness-enforced, and stated as such rather than overclaimed):** `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` *Bash capability boundary* — every agent still carries `Bash`, which subsumes both write and network access regardless of `tools:` / `disallowedTools:`, because the harness's `tools:` field cannot express a scoped Bash pattern (`Bash(gh:*)` is not expressible) and `permissions.allow` / `permissions.deny` patterns — though pattern-capable, and already used once by this package's own `--allow-bundled-scripts` — apply session-wide, never per agent; the only genuinely per-agent mechanism is a `hooks: PreToolUse` validator, a runtime component outside this package's scope. Each agent's own `## Bash boundary` block names the closed purpose its Bash use actually serves and the residual risk left open (#163). Also closed: the memory-filter leak #165 found — the shared brief's folded `## Project memory` section was composed by `Trigger:` match only, so a role-scoped (or worse, personal) entry travelled to every specialist in the dispatch chain regardless of role. The filter now moves to the one channel that is genuinely per-recipient: `daedalus` derives a per-role slice fresh, immediately before each dispatch, and writes it into that dispatch's own `Task` prompt under `## Project memory — <role>`, never into the brief file every specialist shares — the brief itself now carries only a pointer to the mechanism. **Obligation, self-reported, and declared incomplete rather than overclaimed:** an append-only audit trail at `.claude/run/<source-slug>.audit` (mirroring the existing dispatch ledger's lifecycle and append-lock) records memory reads, outbound requests, and external writes; `athena` reads it during CR and raises a Moderate/Critical finding on a discrepancy with the diff, `daedalus` reads the total once before merge, and `talos` renders a mandatory `## Audit` section transcribing it into every PR body. Its declared limitation is stated verbatim rather than implied: the trail is self-reported — a raw `curl` run through `Bash` creates a real outbound request this mechanism cannot capture at the plumbing level until the Bash capability boundary above is harness-enforced rather than advisory (#167). Finally, a single vocabulary and inventory for principle 6 — `## Externally-visible actions & consent levels` in `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` defines **L1** (pre-approved by invocation — e.g. `athena` publishing its review, `talos` opening a Draft PR), **L2** (requires explicit instruction — e.g. `hermes` publishing an announcement, a PR merge), and **L3** (human-only — e.g. every JIRA transition outside the two sanctioned helpers, disclosing an unfixed vulnerability on a public tracker) with a table mapping every agent's externally-visible action to its level and today's gate; five pre-existing formulations (`agents/hermes.md`, `agents/daedalus.md`, `agents/athena.md`, `@rules/jira/general.mdc`) gain an additive `(L1)`/`(L2)`/`(L3)` token each, never a rewrite (#168). **Deferred, not silently dropped** — filed as their own follow-up issues rather than implemented here, because each is either a session-wide default only a consuming project's maintainer should opt into, or a runtime component outside this package's instructions-only scope: an opt-in installer flag writing a session-wide `permissions.deny` for outbound network Bash commands (#184), and an optional per-agent `hooks: PreToolUse` validator enforcing each agent's `## Bash boundary` for real (#185). Issue #160 itself stays open as the umbrella tracking issue until those two follow-ups are resolved.

- ✨ **Added**: the code review now flags **extensive PHPDoc and inline commentary that stands in for readable code** (#179). Issue #53 already flagged a comment whose only content *restates* the code; it said nothing about a comment that is accurate and non-redundant yet large enough that its presence is itself the evidence the code is not readable — a docblock longer than the method body it documents, a multi-paragraph docblock narrating implementation steps that named private methods would state directly, a running step-by-step commentary inside one method body, a block explaining an array shape a DTO would carry in the type system, a comment explaining a condition a well-named predicate would make self-evident. The rationale is staleness: prose next to code drifts, because the code gets refactored and the comment does not, leaving two sources of truth of which only one is executed. So the **Suggested Fix is always the code change** — rename, extract a well-named method, introduce a DTO or value object, split the method — followed by deleting the prose the restructuring made unnecessary; a shorter comment is never the remedy. The heuristic: ask what the comment will say after the next refactor of the code beneath it — if the answer is "something no longer true", the fix belongs in the code. The new bullet owns **volume**, issue #53's owns **redundancy**, and exactly one of the two is raised for the same block. Carve-outs are preserved and extended: *why*-not-*what* prose, a documented residual risk the rules require at the call site (e.g. the SSRF DNS-rebinding acceptance), domain glossaries and ADRs, a `@param` / `@return` carrying a constraint the type system cannot express, navigation markers, vendor-authored docblocks — and, stated explicitly, every comment **this ruleset itself mandates**: the justification on a narrowly-scoped linter suppression and on an unavoidable per-row DB operation are required by other bullets and can never become findings of this one. Severity **Minor**, escalating to **Moderate** when the same diff could have split or renamed instead, so the prose actively substitutes for a structural fix the reviewer can name. `rules/php/core-standards.mdc` *Documentation* carries the standard (*write the code so that extensive PHPDoc and inline commentary are not needed*) and now requires the documentation that genuinely must exist — APIs, complex business rules, non-obvious side effects, constraints — to be stated **concisely** rather than expanded into a narrative the next refactor invalidates. The lens is registered in `skills/code-review/SKILL.md`'s Core Analysis walk-through so it actually runs; that file's pre-existing `body.max_tokens` WARN is unchanged in kind (6 142 → 6 203 against a 5 000 limit). Per user request — *Chci raději čistý a dobře čitelný kod než dokumentaci na x řádků*.

- ✨ **Added**: every change on a branch must belong to a **logical commit** — and the rule now says which one (#179). *One phase = one commit* and *one assignment point = one commit* map the **planned** work; nothing covered what arrives after the plan — a pre-PR review-loop fix, a correction to a commit already made, a follow-through the first pass missed, a CHANGELOG entry — so those landed wherever was convenient: dangling in the working tree, swept into an unrelated commit, or appended as a *"follow-up to the commit above"* that forces a reviewer to read two diffs to see one logical change and breaks the cherry-pick independence the existing rules ask for. `rules/git/general.mdc` now states the decision: a change that **completes or corrects a commit already on the branch is folded into that commit** (`git commit --amend` for the tip, `git commit --fixup=<sha>` + `git rebase --autosquash <base>` for an earlier one), while a **genuinely separate logical unit is a new commit** — and amending is never a way to shrink the commit count, because the count is not the goal, one-logical-change-per-commit is. The guard that keeps this from destroying an in-flight review: once the branch is pushed **and under review** it is not rewritten, since a force-push moves every later SHA, detaches review-thread anchors, invalidates any SHA already cited in a posted report or PR description, and can silently drop a reviewer's in-flight comment; there a new commit naming what it corrects is the correct shape, and a deliberate rewrite publishes with `--force-with-lease` (never plain `--force`) and re-derives every SHA already cited. Reconciliation moves **earlier**: walk `git log <base>..HEAD` against the recorded plan and split a commit that bundled two changes or fold two that turned out to be one **before** opening the PR, while the branch is still yours to rewrite. `skills/resolve-issue/references/phase-planning.md` gains a section 6 carrying the amend-or-new decision table and that reconciliation step, and states that review-loop and CHANGELOG commits stay out of the PR's `## Changes` checklist while still being named in the description, so no commit on the branch is unaccounted for. Per user request — *Všechny změny budou logicky rozdělené do commitů, uprav existující a pokud bude potřeba založ nové commity*.

- 📝 **Changed**: the code review is now **one agent, not two** — `athena` absorbs the entire role of `argos`, and `agents/argos.md` plus its avatar are removed from the roster (#179). The review used to run as two parallel passes on the same PR (`argos` for quality / architecture / optimisation, `athena` for security), which required a shared brief, a per-brief append lock, a barrier held by `daedalus`, a consolidation step to reassemble the two halves into one comment, and — since #174 — an explicit ownership assignment so the two passes would stop deriving the same remediation-conformance verdict twice. All of that apparatus existed to reconstruct a review that a single pass produces directly, and the run behind #172 had already measured what the second pass actually bought: an **identical** verdict over the same six findings, with each pass's genuinely new findings coming from its own lens rather than from the overlap. `athena` now runs the tracker-matching `code-review-*` wrapper (which drives `prepare-issue-context`, `assignment-compliance-check`, `code-review`, `analyze-problem`, `security-review`, `api-review`, `class-refactoring`, the coverage gate and the conditionally-triggered lenses), adds the three security skills that wrapper does **not** run (`laravel-security`, `security-bounty-hunter`, `security-threat-analysis`), applies all three security rule files as the cross-cutting lens, publishes one consolidated review, owns the remediation-conformance verdict whenever a plan exists at all, and drives the `process-code-review` convergence loop. **The `SECURITY_OWNER=athena` flag is now never set by any shipped agent** — it exists to suppress the wrapper's always-run `security-review` step when a *second, concurrent* agent owns that review, and with no peer left, setting it would skip the pass with nothing behind it; the flag and the `security: owned by athena` template slot stay in all four wrappers for an external caller that genuinely runs a concurrent security pass, and the delegation token consequently never appears on a review this roster publishes. `daedalus` dispatches one blocking CR round and reads one handoff: no barrier, no both-handoffs rendezvous, no consolidation re-dispatch. The *Concurrency-safe append* contract and the append lock stay in place and are documented as **dormant** rather than deleted, so a future parallel step needs no retrofit and `athena` keeps taking the lock unconditionally. The two-reviewer clauses that live in **rules** rather than agents — *Remediation-conformance ownership* and *Severity divergence between parallel reviewers* in `rules/code-review/general.mdc`, and the disjoint-invariant split in `rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` *Savings mode* mechanism 1 — are deliberately **kept as the general contract** for a consuming project that does run parallel reviewers, each now stating explicitly that it is a no-op on this roster; deleting them would have exported this package's roster shape as a constraint on every project installing it. The `Role:` vocabulary of the per-project memory format moves from `argos` to `athena` (no entry on disk used the old value). Wording synchronised across `agents/athena.md`, `agents/daedalus.md`, `agents/talos.md`, `agents/apollon.md`, `docs/agents.md`, `README.md` (roster card, the `@athena` usage walkthrough, and the agent count from six to five), `rules/code-review/general.mdc`, `rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc`, `rules/git/general.mdc`, and the three skills that named the loop (`resolve-issue`, `prepare-issue-context`, `pr-deploy-planner`). Both `athena.md` and `daedalus.md` keep exactly **one** sentence naming `argos`, explaining what the consolidation replaced — a test asserts that count and that no other agent mentions it at all.

- ✨ **Added**: the review's scope is now an explicit, agent-level contract: **`athena` reads the diff of the current changes and nothing else** (#179). The bound already existed per-lens — *"Pre-existing complexity the diff does not touch is out of scope"*, *"Existing methods that the diff does not touch are out of scope"* — but never as one statement the whole pass is held to, so nothing stopped a lens from widening into a repository audit. The new `## Review scope — the current diff only` section states that the subject is `git diff <base>..<head>`; that reading callers, base classes, config, migrations and existing tests *around* the diff stays **required**, because that is how a finding gets grounded in real code, while the surrounding code is never itself a source of findings; that every published `Critical` / `Moderate` / `Minor` anchors to a `file:line` the diff touched, and a pre-existing surface enters scope only once the diff touches it; that the Laravel architecture walk covering every section of `@rules/laravel/architecture.mdc` is a full walk of the **rules** against the diff, not a full audit of the **repository**; and that the whole-app `laravel-security` audit workflow runs only when a caller explicitly asks for one, never as part of reviewing a PR. This is also what makes the three-iteration cap affordable — each round re-reads a diff, not a codebase. The cap itself needed no change: `maxIterations = 3` has been the loop's safety net since #62 and is pinned in `skills/process-code-review/SKILL.md`, `agents/daedalus.md`, `docs/agents.md`, and `rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc`.

- ✨ **Added**: out-of-scope findings are now **filed as issues on the tracker**, not just written into a PR comment (#179). A *Refactoring Proposals* entry is a real problem that the current PR is not the place to fix, and until now it lived only inside the review comment — where it disappears the moment the PR merges and nobody re-reads the thread. `athena` now also opens one issue per out-of-scope item, so the deferred work is queued rather than merely observed. **What is filed:** each *Refactoring Proposals* entry plus each out-of-scope assignment-traceability finding whose remedy is *"move it to its own issue / PR"*. An in-scope Critical / Moderate / Minor is never deferred this way — it is fixed in this PR and still counts toward the convergence gate. **Where:** the tracker the source resolved to, never a hardcoded channel — the PR's own repository for a GitHub source, the key's project for JIRA, the repository behind the error's `linkedIssues[]` for Bugsnag, and nothing at all when no source resolves (the list travels back in the handoff instead). **How:** through the existing `@skills/create-issue/SKILL.md`, which already owns issue formatting and the *never invent a label* rule, rather than a second issue-creation path. Two properties keep it from becoming noise or a blocker: filing happens **once per run at publish time**, not once per loop iteration (three rounds would otherwise file the same proposal three times) and is deduplicated against the tracker's open issues, recording `already filed: <link>` on a match; and a **filing failure never blocks the review** — it is logged in the handoff with the full list inline and convergence proceeds, because queueing follow-up work is not a gate on the change under review. **A security out-of-scope item is never filed on a public tracker**: `code-review` routes out-of-scope security shortcomings into the same *Refactoring Proposals* section (its own example is *"missing webhook signature verification across other consumers"*), so filing that section wholesale would publish a live, unpatched weakness to every reader of a public repository. Filing therefore checks tracker visibility first (`gh repo view --json isPrivate`; a private tracker is not a disclosure surface) and, on a public one, routes such an item to the project's private security channel when one exists or withholds it with the reason stated in the handoff so a human owns the disclosure decision — withholding never drops it, the item still appears in the review comment. The filed links become a first-class handoff field.

- 📝 **Changed**: every agent that ran at `effort: max` now runs at **`effort: high`** (#179), superseding the `effort: max` mandate from #40 — `athena`, `daedalus`, `talos`, and `hermes`. `apollon` stays at `effort: low` by explicit decision: its scoped validation gate is deliberately fast and cheap, so raising it to `high` would have worked against the token-saving motivation of this issue rather than for it. `docs/agents.md` *Anatomy of an agent* documents `high` as the level to set, names the `apollon` exception, and its frontmatter example is updated in lockstep; the frontmatter test asserts `high` on every agent but `apollon` **and** that `effort: max` survives on none of them.

- ✨ **Added**: `resolve-issue` now derives its commit plan from the **points the assignment enumerates**, not only from phases, so a PR's commit list is the list of resolved points. The old planning step recognised `Phase N` headings, numbered milestones, and ordered acceptance criteria — but the most common shape of a real assignment is a list of *recommended fixes* (a review's findings with their suggested fixes, a checklist, a bulleted defect list), and none of those matched a phase marker, so the whole assignment landed as one opaque commit and a reviewer had to reconstruct from the diff which of the listed points were actually resolved. `rules/git/general.mdc` gains **One assignment point = one commit** (the same rule as one phase = one commit, applied to an enumeration of points — deduplicate points describing the same change, split a point bundling two unrelated changes, and never let a deferred or pre-existing point become an in-scope commit) plus **Prefer independent, cherry-pickable commits**: disjoint file sets over two commits editing the same lines, shared groundwork in the commit of the point that introduces it, and a stated `depends on #N` when two points genuinely cannot be separated. Independence is deliberately a **preference, not a gate** — a hard requirement would push a run to merge two points into one commit or invent an artificial split, which is exactly the readability the change is buying. `skills/resolve-issue/references/phase-planning.md` carries the procedure in five steps (inventory the point markers → map one point to one commit, each complete with its own tests → order for cherry-pick → record the numbered commit-plan table *before* implementing → implement point by point, committing at each point's end), and defines how that table renders as the PR's new `## Changes` section: one line per commit, never per file, naming the point in the assignment's own wording and marking dependencies, with pre-existing fixes and deferred items staying in their existing `## Pre-existing fixes` / `## TODO` sections. The skill body absorbs this **token-neutrally** — it sat at exactly the 5000-token skill-check limit, so the point-marker list and the cherry-pick clause are paid for by delegating the phase-detection detail to the reference file the section already pointed at, and the body ends at 4997. Three new tests in `tests/Installer/CompoundEngineeringContentTest.php` pin the two rule clauses, the point markers in the body, the four procedure sections the CR can regress (§1–§4; §5 carries no pinned literal), the nesting-precedence rule, and the PR change-list shape including its review-loop carve-out.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: the merge skill's code-review staleness gate justified itself with a premise the repository had already retired. `skills/merge-github-pr/SKILL.md` told the reader to compare `updatedAt` against the head commit *"because the CR comment is upserted in place — follow-up runs edit the same comment"*, but every CR run now POSTs a fresh comment and the helper's PATCH branch is gone (`skills/code-review-github/scripts/upsert-comment.sh`, asserted by `tests/Installer/CodeReviewContentTest.php`). The gate still passed in practice — on a comment nobody edits, `createdAt == updatedAt` — so this was reasoning that no longer matched the mechanism, not a live bug; left standing, the next person to touch the gate would have reasoned from a convention that does not exist. It now reads **`createdAt`**, which under the always-new-comment convention is when the review actually ran. That is also the **stricter** of the two fields (`createdAt <= updatedAt`), so the change can only reject more reviews, never accept one it previously refused: a later cosmetic edit to a comment's body can no longer make a review that ran against an older diff look current. The same field was corrected in the *delegated security coverage* gate below it, which inherits the rule verbatim, and in `agents/daedalus.md`, which quotes the gate when it decides whether an unmet CR gate is repairable. Also fixed the sentence in `skills/code-review/SKILL.md` Output Rules that still claimed cross-run history "is preserved by the tracker's edit history on the upserted comment" — it contradicted the *Cross-run history* section earlier in that same file, and now matches the wording the three wrappers already carried.

- ✨ **Added**: from the **third** CR iteration onward the review reports **Critical and Moderate findings only** — the non-blocking output is dropped. The `process-code-review` review loop fixes Critical / Moderate findings exclusively, so by iteration 3 every Minor nit and refactoring proposal in the report has been published and read twice without being acted on, and it does nothing but bury the blockers still standing between the PR and its merge. The new `rules/code-review/general.mdc` *Late-Iteration Report Scope — Critical & Moderate Only (CR iteration > 2)* section owns the contract; `process-code-review` now passes an explicit `iteration = <N>` to the CR wrapper on **every** invocation — the quiet loop runs and the final publishing run, which carries the loop's **final** iteration number so a loop that needed more than two rounds also publishes the narrowed report. **Dropped:** every Minor finding wherever it renders (`## Findings`, `## Architecture`, `## Database Analysis`), `## Refactoring (DRY / tech debt)`, `## Refactoring proposals`. **Kept:** `## Excluded per assignment`, because the Exclusion Gate requires that every finding it relocates is recorded and never silently dropped — that is an audit record, not a finding. Three properties make the narrowing safe rather than a quality cut: it is a **rendering filter** applied at Output assembly (no walk-through, specialized review, or verification step is skipped, so a later `iteration = 1` run reproduces the dropped items in full); the **counts stay truthful** (#74) — the `Counts:` line keeps the real detected numbers, never zeroed to match what is rendered, and a new `Report scope:` header line names the suppression, so a suppressed Minor is visible as a number and can never read as a clean one; and the **convergence gate is untouched** at `Critical + Moderate == 0`, i.e. exactly the two severities the narrowed report keeps. Absent an explicit `iteration` — a standalone CR run, a pre-PR self-check, a first review — the run is treated as iteration 1 and renders the full report, so nothing narrows by default. Wired through all four wrappers (`code-review`, `code-review-github`, `code-review-jira`, `code-review-bugsnag`) and their four output templates.

- 📝 **Changed**: verifying that a *pre-implementation* analysis's findings were actually remediated is now derived **once per PR head SHA by exactly one reviewer** instead of independently by both (#174, deciding the item 5 deferred from #172). The owner's condition on #174 was "split it if token consumption will be lower, otherwise leave it": with the duplication live today being one extra derivation per CR round and the fix adding **no** new dispatch step (it is an ownership assignment, not a pipeline step), consumption is strictly lower, so the split lands. Read/Map/Verify moved the target first — the waste is not in a skill both reviewers run, because `athena`'s security-review mode runs only the four security skills and never touches the assignment-conformance gate; **nothing anywhere defined who verifies the remediation items**, so both reviewers did. On the run behind #172 every pass independently re-read the same guard, architecture tests, disk config, and prune command over the same six findings and reached an **identical** verdict, while the findings each pass actually contributed came from its own lens. Undefined work that everyone does is the root cause, so the fix is ownership: `athena` owns the verdict when the plan was her security-risk analysis, `argos` otherwise (or the step is empty when no plan preceded), `daedalus` names the owner in both dispatch prompts because it is the only participant that knows whether the analysis step ran, and the non-owner spends its budget on its own lens. No check is removed — the verdict is still produced once, by the reviewer best placed to produce it, and an unaddressed item still blocks convergence at the severity the original analysis assigned. The redundancy loss is bounded deliberately, because #174 itself named the risk of a single unchallenged source of truth: the non-owner may re-verify **one** entry when it has concrete reason to doubt it (the entry contradicts the diff, or its cited `file:line` does not support the verdict) and records the divergence, which the severity-divergence tie-break from #172 then resolves — blanket re-derivation "to be safe" is exactly what the split removes. Verdicts are keyed to the head SHA, so one is never carried across new commits. Also worth recording against the #172 framing: that issue counted **four** duplicate derivations, but two of them existed only because of items 2 and 3 (a re-dispatched round and an undelivered pass) which #173 already fixed — the saving this change adds is the remaining one-per-round, not the full four. Two new tests across `tests/Installer/CodeReviewContentTest.php` and `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php`, carrying five pinned literals each verified to fail when the wording it pins is removed.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: orchestration overhead was doubling the cost and wall-clock of a `/resolve-issue` run without adding any analytical depth — five of the six defects measured in #172 are closed (~1.4 M of that run's 2.2 M tokens were pure overhead). **`daedalus` dispatched fire-and-forget.** Nothing in `agents/daedalus.md` mentioned that Task dispatches run in the background by default, so a `dispatched` return read as a completed step: the turn ended with no progress, the parent had to wake the agent, and each resume re-read a brief that had grown to ~1 200 lines. Three such cycles cost **838 k tokens** and produced nothing past `dispatched` — more than any individual reviewer pass (the largest was 429 k), though less than the four of them combined (1.36 M). Every step now dispatches with `run_in_background: false`, justified by the observation that no step of the pipeline yields a result `daedalus` does not consume, and the step-6 barrier collapses into a single turn: the `argos` ‖ `athena` pair is two blocking calls in one message, which keeps the concurrency and still ends the turn holding both handoffs. A harness that cannot honour a blocking dispatch is a named blocker, never a narrated plan — owning the sequencing while being unable to finish it is the one state the rule forbids. **No dispatch idempotence.** A specialist appends its brief section only when it *finishes*, so between dispatch and handoff there is no trace at all and a resumed run re-derives "no handoff" as "not dispatched": one CR round went out three times and the brief ended with two `athena` and two `argos` sections, one explicitly annulling the other. A ledger at `.claude/run/<source-slug>.dispatches` keyed `{role, pr-head-sha, round}` now records `dispatched` / `delivered` / `failed` — append-only lines rather than a JSON document, because two parallel writers doing read-modify-write lose whichever transition landed first, and the head SHA is what makes a round after new commits a genuinely new round instead of a repeat. **Findings that could not be delivered.** Two completed CR passes delivered nothing because `upsert-comment.sh` and the brief append both need `Bash`, and the isolated worktree they ran in had been removed underneath them (`working directory no longer exists`); ~590 k tokens of finished analysis survived only because a human copied it out of the handoff. Both reviewers now append a section skeleton as soon as scope is settled and add findings incrementally, and treat the **returned handoff** as the authoritative delivery — carrying the full findings in it rather than a pointer to the brief, and saying so when an append or publish failed. This departs from the issue's proposal of a `.pending/` pickup file written with "an available file-write tool": neither agent holds a `Write` tool (Read/Glob/Grep/Bash/WebSearch/WebFetch), so when `Bash` dies no file is reachable and the proposal is not implementable as written; the handoff is the one channel that cannot fail, because returning it *is* the agent's contract with the harness, and it is what actually rescued the measured run. Granting `Write` was considered and rejected — `Bash` already subsumes write capability, so it would widen the declared surface without adding a channel. **Cleanup killing live reviewers.** The startup sweep already probes the `pid <N>` token in a CR worktree's lock reason and treats anything ambiguous as alive, but step 7's cleanup checked only "not the active tree, no uncommitted changes" — and a live CR pass is indistinguishable from a crashed one on both signals, which is how the worktrees above were removed mid-review. Cleanup now applies the sweep's own confirmed-dead gate; the issue proposed a heartbeat file, but the probe this file already defines and the sweep already exercises covers the same ground without a second mechanism to keep in sync. **Undefined severity on divergence.** The same defect was `Critical` to `argos` and `Moderate` to `athena`, both published, one naming the divergence in an addendum; the convergence gate is defined purely in Critical/Moderate counts, so nothing said which severity the merged review carries — and a divergence across the blocking boundary would leave convergence genuinely undefined. `rules/code-review/general.mdc` now resolves it: the **higher** severity wins (a conservative gate must not be relaxed by the lower of two reads), the defect is reported once at that severity, and the divergence plus its resolution is recorded in both handoffs. Item 5 of the issue — splitting the CR into one conformance pass plus parallel discovery — is **deliberately not implemented**: the issue itself flags items 5 and 6 as methodology changes to assess separately and calls 5 an explicit trade-off rather than a clear win, so it is filed as its own decision issue instead of being picked silently. Six new tests across `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php` and `tests/Installer/CodeReviewContentTest.php`, each verified to fail when the wording it pins is removed.

- 📝 **Changed**: the eight Minor findings deferred from the consolidated `argos` + `athena` review of #149 are resolved in `rules/sql/optimalize.mdc` and `skills/postgres-patterns/SKILL.md` (#156). **Discoverability:** the MySQL 8.0.16+ / InnoDB scope statement that governs the whole schema block moves from under the `## Strict SQL Mode` heading — where a reader looking for a version scope would never find it — to the top of `## Schema Design`, where the block starts, and carries the explicit MySQL narrowing it lacked; `## Strict SQL Mode` keeps the half that is genuinely about `sql_mode`. The `Fitting data types` line (`INT`, `DECIMAL`, `VARCHAR(n)`, `DATETIME`) now says it is a starting point rather than the permitted set and names the sections mandating `DATE`, `BIGINT`, `TEXT`/`MEDIUMTEXT`, `ENUM`, and `CHAR(n)`, since a reader treating it as exhaustive stores a calendar day as `DATETIME` — the exact bug the date section warns about. **Convention:** the three ```sql blocks added by #149 were fragments (a column definition with a trailing comma, a bare `CONSTRAINT`, a bare `CHECK`) while every pre-existing block in the file is a runnable statement; they become a `CREATE TABLE` and two `ALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT` statements, guarded not by three verbatim pins but by a parser over every ```sql block in the file asserting the first non-comment line opens a statement and the last does not dangle a comma — so the next fragment fails regardless of wording. **Recognisability:** `Charset Choice for Externally-Queried Columns` gains the two properties that let a reader match the error in front of them against the rule — every string operator fails rather than just `=`, and the compared string decides the failure while the table contents decide nothing, so an empty table errors identically. `CONVERT(? USING ascii)` is named only as the escape hatch that must be repeated at every call site, which is why it is not the fix, per the constraint the security review attached to that finding; a test pins that framing so a later edit cannot promote it to advice. **Security:** permission and verification booleans (`can_*`, `is_admin`, `*_verified`, `two_factor_enabled`) now state that the safe default is the restrictive `DEFAULT 0`, since the section's only example was the permissive `enabled DEFAULT 1`; `Primary Key Sizing` states that a sequential key is enumerable and its unguessability is never a control (authorize every lookup, expose a separate non-sequential public identifier only where set size or membership is itself sensitive, and do not swap the PK for a UUID — that pays the InnoDB index cost the same section quantifies for a property that was never a defence); and the `CREATE TRIGGER` example sets `DEFINER` explicitly, because MySQL has no INVOKER mode for triggers so an omitted clause binds the trigger to whichever privileged account ran the migration and carries that into every dump — as `DEFINER = CURRENT_USER`, with the two constraints that decide how the account is named spelled out beside it, since the naive "name a least-privilege account" advice is unreachable without `SET_ANY_DEFINER` on the creator and would push a project into granting the one privilege that lets anyone who can land a migration run statements as any account. **Postgres:** `postgres-patterns` no longer defers the schema-design block to a MySQL-scoped rule — that block's mandates are the opposite advice there (`TIMESTAMP` is banned in MySQL for a 2038 limit Postgres does not have, `UNSIGNED` and `utf8mb4` do not exist) — while the engine-neutral half stays deferred, so the exclusion is scoped rather than a blanket opt-out. Every MySQL claim was measured against 8.4.6 rather than asserted: the operator sweep and both error codes (1267 for `=`/`<>`/`>`/`LIKE`/`IN`/`REGEXP`/`CONCAT`/`GREATEST`, **3854** for `LOCATE`/`REPLACE` — a code the file did not mention at all, which is exactly where a reader concludes they have a different bug), the empty-table behaviour, all three rewritten DDL statements plus the order `CHECK` actually firing, and `SQL SECURITY INVOKER` being a syntax error on `CREATE TRIGGER`. That last probe also caught the half of the trigger finding that would have made the advice wrong: a least-privilege definer lacking the `TRIGGER` privilege on the table makes **every** write to that table fail with `ERROR 1142`, whoever issues it — so the rule ships with the grant it requires. Eleven new tests across `tests/Installer/SqlRulesContentTest.php` and `tests/Installer/SkillsContentTest.php`, verified falsifiable by restoring the original wording. The ninth Minor from that review (the `'cs_CZ'` claim in the `Collation` section) was already corrected before #149 merged.

- ✨ **Added**: SSRF is now a checkable rule rather than a policy statement (#169). `rules/security/backend.md` *External Requests* already said the right things — allow-list destinations, validate user-supplied URLs — but said them in five generic bullets with no call patterns to match, no severity, and no fix shape; a reviewer could agree with every word and still walk past `Http::get($request->url)`. The new **Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF)** section supplies the checkable half: the sinks to grep for (`Http::*`, Guzzle, cURL, and the stream wrappers that are outbound requests in disguise — `file_get_contents`, `fopen`, `copy`, `getimagesize`, `simplexml_load_file`, `DOMDocument::load`), the feature shapes that carry them (webhook-callback registration, avatar / OG-preview fetchers, "import from URL"), and the five controls a sink needs before it stops being a finding. Two of those are the ones most often missing in practice: **redirects**, because both the Laravel HTTP client and Guzzle follow them by default, so a URL that passes every check can `302` to an internal one and a validated first hop proves nothing; and **post-resolution range rejection**, because validating the host *string* misses a public-looking hostname that resolves to loopback, RFC-1918, or the cloud-metadata address `169.254.169.254`. DNS rebinding is named as a residual risk rather than waved away — the check-then-reconnect gap is real, closing it needs a pinned address or an egress proxy, and accepting it has to be stated at the sink. Severity splits **Critical** (unauthenticated or low-privilege reach, observable response, or metadata / internal admin reachable) from **High** (everything else, including blind SSRF). Both Laravel claims were verified at the source rather than inferred: the docs page for the HTTP client documents `allow_redirects` but never mentions `withoutRedirecting()`, so its existence and semantics (`$this->options['allow_redirects'] = false`) were confirmed in `Illuminate\Http\Client\PendingRequest` on the 12.x branch. That check also surfaced the better posture the rule now recommends — flipping the default once via `Http::globalOptions(['allow_redirects' => false])` in `AppServiceProvider::boot()` and opting back in per call site, so a new sink is safe by default instead of safe only when its author remembered — plus the near-miss it warns against: `maxRedirects()` caps the hop count but still follows the first hop into the private network. The **Suggested Fix** routes every user-influenced URL through one central validator instead of repeating checks per call site, and cites this repository's own precedent — the outbound documentation lookup in `skills/code-review/SKILL.md` already allows only public `https://` vendor hosts and names the same forbidden ranges. `skills/security-review/SKILL.md` gains the matching walk, and its pre-existing *External Interaction (APIs & SSRF)* checklist now hands off to it with a **binding** scope boundary rather than a hint: five of its seven bullets are the SSRF surface the walk owns and must never produce a finding alongside it for the same line, while the two the walk does not subsume — rate limiting / abuse protection and the third-party API contract — stay the checklist's own. Scope boundaries stop double-reporting: disabled TLS on the same request stays with issue #549, and a user-supplied browser redirect target stays an open redirect under the frontend rule. The frontend and mobile mirrors follow the established shape — the control is server-side, client-side URL checks are UX only, and Node / Electron / build tooling counts as a server for this purpose. Four new tests in `tests/Installer/SecurityContentTest.php` pin the sinks, both validation controls, the redirect clause, the fix shape, and the hand-off, and were verified to fail when the pinned wording is removed. **Decision:** the issue offered either extending `security-review` or a standalone `ssrf-review` skill; extending won, because `security-review` already runs on every code review (so the check is automatic, which the issue asked for) while a second skill would have to be added to three CR wrappers' always-run lists plus `athena`'s roster, and splitting one OWASP category across two skills invites the duplicate findings the scope-boundary convention exists to prevent.

- ✨ **Added**: `vendor/bin/ai-olympus resolve-next` hands the **oldest unclaimed** open issue carrying the configured labels to Claude Code as one agent run, so a `cron` entry (or Windows Task Scheduler) can work the backlog unattended (#158). The run chains `/resolve-issue` → `/code-review-github` → `/process-code-review` on the issue it picked. **Merging is opt-in:** without `--merge` the generated prompt explicitly instructs the agent to leave the pull request open for human review, so an unattended schedule never merges on its own — the issue asked for exactly that boundary. Options: `--label=NAME` (repeatable, all must match; defaults to `Resolve_by_AI`), `--repo=OWNER/NAME`, `--merge`, `--dry-run`. An issue already carrying `Resolve_by_AI:in-progress` is skipped, so two overlapping ticks cannot collide on the same issue, and an empty backlog exits `0` because a quiet schedule is not a failure. **Language choice:** PHP, as a subcommand of the CLI the package already ships — the plugin is a Composer package, so PHP is a dependency the user necessarily has, `bin/ai-olympus` already lands in `vendor/bin/` of every consuming project, and the logic is covered by the existing Pest suite under the same 100%-coverage gate; a Node or Python script would have added a runtime for no gain. Cross-platform behaviour comes from invoking `proc_open()` with an **array** command, which bypasses the shell entirely, so no per-OS quoting rule applies and a label or prompt containing spaces cannot be reinterpreted. The process layer is the one thing that is *not* in `src/`: `@rules/code-testing/general.mdc` forbids a test from spawning an external binary, so `bin/ai-olympus` builds the executor closure and `src/Cli.php` takes it as a required argument — every line under `src/` therefore stays reachable from a test with a fake, and the 25 new tests in `tests/Installer/ResolveNextTest.php` assert the exact `gh` and `claude` command lines without ever running either. Payload fields from the CLI are type-validated rather than cast, so a missing or wrongly-typed field degrades to a harmless placeholder instead of a silent coercion.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: pre-existing — `bin/ai-olympus` discarded the exit code it was given. The script ended in a bare `Installer::run($argv);`, so PHP exited `0` no matter what the installer returned: a failing `vendor/bin/ai-olympus install` reported success to the shell, and any CI step or `cron` entry checking `$?` could never see it. Verified directly (`php -r '… Installer::run(["x","bogus"]);'` exits `0`; the same call wrapped in `exit(...)` exits `1`). The dispatcher call is now wrapped in `exit(...)`. The fix is inseparable from the #158 line that replaced that statement, so it rides in the same commit rather than a standalone one, and is pinned by a regression test asserting the wrapper is present and the bare call has not come back — pinned by reading the file, since the testing rules forbid a test from executing the binary. This mattered immediately for #158: an unattended schedule that cannot detect its own failures is worse than no schedule.

- ✨ **Added**: `README.md` now carries a browsable **Skill Catalog** of all 50 skills, grouped into ten sections by what a reader reaches for them for — *Issue → PR workflow*, *Code review*, *Security*, *Testing*, *Databases*, *Frontend & UI*, *Infrastructure & performance*, *Refactoring & code quality*, *Analysis & planning*, *Meta & tooling* (#104). The README previously advertised the skill count and then named none of them, so a visitor could not tell whether the package solved their problem without cloning the repository, and the single largest discoverability surface — 50 names and descriptions that GitHub search, Google, and LLM crawlers all index — did not exist. Each row links to `skills/<name>/` and carries the skill's **own `description:` front-matter**, mechanically trimmed to one line: the `Use when` prefix is dropped so the column reads as a statement, and the summary is cut at the detail list (an em dash), the alternative trigger (`, or when`), or the end of the first sentence. The derived text is always a substring of what the skill itself declares, which is what makes *"no invented capabilities"* a checkable property rather than a promise. The catalog sits **after** the agent roster, per the issue's ordering — agents are the hook, skills are the depth — so the first-screen experience rebuilt in #105 is untouched. The issue's fallback of moving the list to `docs/skills.md` was **deliberately not taken**: the catalog's whole rationale is that the README is the indexed surface, and at 104 lines on a 388-line file it does not stretch it disproportionately. Five tests in `tests/Installer/SkillCatalogContentTest.php` pin all four acceptance criteria — every skill directory present exactly once, every link resolving to a directory that really holds a `SKILL.md`, every row's label matching its own href (so a rename cannot relabel a row while still pointing at the old directory), the advertised count equal to the row count, and every description byte-identical to the value re-derived from front-matter by `skillCatalogDescription()`. That last one was verified falsifiable before landing: planting an invented capability and a mismatched link in the catalog failed three of the five tests. Note that the issue was written against a 48-skill snapshot; the repository ships **50**, and both the prose and the tests read the live count rather than a hardcoded number.

- ✨ **Added**: `rules/code-testing/general.mdc` now bans tautological assertions outright — *"no tautological assertion belongs in the codebase"*. A tautology is an assertion that holds no matter what the code under test does; it is worse than a missing test, because a gap is visible while a tautology counts toward coverage and reads as verified behaviour. The new **No Tautological Assertions** section names the six detectable forms — a literal asserted against itself, a value the test itself just assigned with no call to the system under test in between, a re-asserted configured test double, a language or framework guarantee the type system already enforces, an expected value computed by the code under test (so both sides move together), and a test with no assertion at all that passes merely because nothing threw — each with the case that is *not* a violation (a named constructor / cast / normaliser that genuinely transformed the input; an explicit `not->toThrow()` when "does not throw" is the contract). It closes with the falsifiability test the whole rule reduces to: break the production code the test claims to cover, and if the test still passes, the assertion is a tautology. CR severity is **Moderate**, escalating to **Critical** when the tautology is the only assertion covering a changed line — the change then ships untested while reporting as covered. The rule is **enforced, not merely documented**: two guards in `tests/Installer/CodeTestingContentTest.php` scan the suite itself for the two mechanically decidable forms (a constant on the left of an equality/truthiness matcher, and a test block containing no assertion), and both were proven to fail against a deliberately planted violation before being committed — the suite was clean at 434 test blocks across 20 files when the rule landed, so it is introduced as a true statement rather than an aspiration. Requested directly by the maintainer, outside the scope of #105.

- 📝 **Documented**: the top third of `README.md` is rebuilt for a first-time reader (#105). The file opened with a 150-word paragraph that mixed the value proposition with `composer.json` project-root discovery, directory mirroring, and symlink behaviour, and a visitor then had to scroll past roughly 70 lines of auto-install configuration, command listings, the 7-step installer flow, and the CLI switches table before reaching the agent roster — the strongest content the README has. The intro is now a three-sentence hero (what it is, who it is for, what it replaces, with no installer mechanics), a **Quickstart** carrying the two install commands plus one concrete first prompt (`@daedalus resolve …`) so the reader sees a result rather than a setup, a three-row **What You Get** table mapping Rules / Skills / Agents onto where each lands (the mapping already existed, buried under the installer), and a **Why This Package** list rewritten from internal properties to user outcomes — the bullet describing the package's own code style (`typed properties, SRP, no redundant comments`) said nothing to someone deciding whether to install it. **Nothing was deleted:** the operational reference moved verbatim into the new `docs/installation.md`, together with the installer-mechanics sentence dropped from the hero. `Available Commands` moved along with `CLI Switches` even though the issue names only the latter — the table documents the flags the list uses, so splitting them would have left the command list referenced nowhere. The flag-coverage guarantee from issue #102 (`tests/Installer/CliRunTest.php`) is **re-pointed, not weakened**: it still derives flag names by reflection over `InstallOptions` and still requires each one in both the command list and the switches table, now reading `docs/installation.md`; its section slicing no longer depends on the switches table being followed by a `---` rule, since the new `installerDocsSection()` helper slices to the next same-level heading or end of file. Five new tests in `tests/Installer/ReadmeContentTest.php` pin the acceptance criteria that were previously unenforceable: the install command inside the first 40 rendered lines, a hero free of installer mechanics, every moved section present in `docs/installation.md`, every relative Markdown link resolving to a path that exists, and every in-page anchor resolving to one of the README's own headings — the last two are new guarantees the repository did not have at all.

- ✨ **Added**: `rules/sql/optimalize.mdc` now covers the MySQL schema-design rules from ["MySQL: deset pravidel pro schéma, které nezradí"](https://phpfashion.com/cs/deset-pravidel-pro-navrh-mysql-schematu) (David Grudl, phpFashion) that the file did not yet carry — 15 new sections: **Strict SQL Mode** (the `sql_mode` prerequisite, set server-side and from day one), **Naming Conventions** (singular table names, `id`/`<singular>_id`/`parent_id` PK-FK convention, module prefixes, unit-bearing column names), **Nullability and Defaults** (`NULL` vs `''` semantics, the `NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` convention, `NULL` comparison gotchas, `DEFAULT` as a domain claim not a crash guard), **Date and Time Column Types** (`_at`/`_date` suffix convention, the calendar-day-stored-as-`datetime` timezone bug), **modified_at vs updated_at** (domain-change trigger vs. concurrency-token semantics, with a trigger example), **Boolean Columns** (a naming decision procedure ending in `is_`/`can_` as a legitimate fallback), **String, Text, and ENUM Types** (the `VARCHAR(255)` cargo-cult history, `TEXT` byte-vs-character size, `ENUM` trade-offs), **Money and Decimal Types** (`FLOAT`/`DOUBLE` banned, `DECIMAL` scale by domain, non-negativity via `CHECK` not deprecated `UNSIGNED`), **Foreign Key Actions** (`ON UPDATE CASCADE` default, `ON DELETE` decided by composition/association/meaningful-disconnect), **CHECK Constraints** (invariant vs. policy, the `NOT A OR B` conditional-obligation pattern, naming constraints after the rule), **Collation** (`utf8mb4_0900_*` family, the collation-dependent `CHECK`/`REGEXP` gotcha), **Charset Choice for Externally-Queried Columns** (an ASCII-charset column compared against attacker-influenced input throws `ERROR 1267` instead of returning empty, turning an app-level 404 into a raw 500 — cross-referenced to `@rules/security/backend.md`'s existing "error handling without revealing sensitive information" principle without duplicating it there), **Primary Key Sizing** (`INT UNSIGNED AUTO_INCREMENT` default, `BIGINT` for append-only tables, the FK-type-propagation cost), **Index and Constraint Naming** (explicit naming, index-vs-constraint prefix convention), and **When to Break These Rules** (a documented-`COMMENT` exception policy). The pre-existing `Schema Design` list ("Fitting data types: `INT`, `DECIMAL`, `VARCHAR(n)`, `TIMESTAMP`") is reconciled to say `DATETIME` instead of `TIMESTAMP`, since the newly added rule against `TIMESTAMP` (year-2038 range limit, silent per-connection timezone conversion) would otherwise directly contradict it. Everything the file already covered (leftmost-prefix composite indexes, low-cardinality index avoidance, dropping unused indexes, the bare `lower_case_snake_case` / `INT`/`DECIMAL`/`VARCHAR(n)` mentions) is left untouched, not restated. One factual claim from a later review round was corrected before merge: the `Collation` section stated that a case-insensitive `_ai_ci` collation makes `CHECK (lang REGEXP '^[a-z]{2}$')` accept both `'CS'` and `'cs_CZ'`. Verified empirically against MySQL 8.4.6 — `'CS'` matches (1), `'cs_CZ'` does not (0): collation drives case folding only, never anchors or quantifiers, so the length bound holds regardless. The sentence now scopes the trap to case alone and says so explicitly, with a pinning test carrying the negative assertion. Pinned by 19 new tests in `tests/Installer/SqlRulesContentTest.php`. Per user request — *Uprav mysql skilly a pravidla tak, ze si prectes clanek https://phpfashion.com/cs/deset-pravidel-pro-navrh-mysql-schematu a chybejici informace doplnis do vhodnych souboru!*.

- 📝 **Documented**: `skills/process-code-review/SKILL.md`'s own loop-gate paragraph now **cites** the canonical staleness guard instead of restating it (#143, deferred from the `argos` review on PR #139 as "Refactoring proposal 1"). The sentence previously read *"requires a clean working tree with `HEAD` at the exact commit CI validated"* — a fourth independent restatement of the CI-reuse staleness concept, and the only one left without the *"actually-checked-out SHA (not merely the workflow's nominal trigger SHA — a `pull_request` event may check out a merge ref)"* caveat that issue #137 added to `rules/code-review/general.mdc`, `rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc`, `agents/argos.md`, and `agents/athena.md`. It now points at the **Staleness guard** bullet of the subsection it already cites, which in turn cites the canonical sentence in `@rules/code-review/general.mdc` *Validation & Coverage Gate* → Coverage gate → "Reuse CI results when available" for the definition — the same citation-not-restatement pattern `skills/resolve-issue/references/quality-gates.md` adopted in #137 — while keeping the two conditions a reader of this file needs inline: the clean tree, and the loop gate's extra requirement that the local `HEAD` equal that SHA (a locally committed but unpushed fix leaves a clean tree while `headRefOid` still points at the previous commit). Behaviour is unchanged; only the drift surface shrinks — the caveat can no longer be hardened in five places and silently missed in one. The pinning test issue #137 added for the old wording is replaced by one that asserts the citation, the caveat, and both retained conditions, plus a **negative** assertion that the removed restatement does not creep back in beside the citation.

- 📝 **Documented**: `skills/resolve-issue/references/quality-gates.md` now records that the CI-result reuse mechanism from issue #124 is **structurally unreachable in this repository** (#144, deferred from the `argos` review on PR #139). `.github/workflows/pr.yml` is `pull_request`-triggered with a bare `actions/checkout@v4` and no `ref: ${{ github.event.pull_request.head.sha }}`, so a PR run checks out the merge ref (`refs/pull/<N>/merge`) while reporting the PR head as `head_sha`; the Staleness guard matches against the run's actually-checked-out SHA, so the match can never hold and every check always runs locally here. The note scopes the consequence to the **PR side** — the same workflow's `push: branches: [master]` trigger does check out the pushed commit — and states why the workflow is **deliberately not** changed to pin the head SHA: doing so would trade a CI signal validating the *merged* result for one validating the branch in isolation, in exchange for an optimization the guard's own clean-tree requirement already makes near-unreachable (reuse fires only on a loop iteration that changed nothing since the cited run, and never on `resolve-issue`'s pre-PR gate, which precedes any CI result). The reuse path remains useful for a consuming project whose CI explicitly checks out the head SHA. The accompanying test pins the workflow shape the note depends on — trigger, bare checkout, absence of a head-SHA `ref:` — not just the prose, so a later workflow change cannot silently leave a false claim in the docs. Wording deliberately avoids restating the guard's definition, which issue #137 established must be **cited** from `@rules/code-review/general.mdc` rather than duplicated here; the negative pin from that issue caught the first draft doing exactly that. The CR on PR #153 then tightened two things: the workflow pin now asserts the **absence of any `ref:`** on the checkout step rather than forbidding one literal expression (the first version stayed green against `ref: ${{ github.head_ref }}`, the likelier of the two edits, so the safeguard was weaker than advertised), and the note now **cites the vendor documentation** its premise rests on — the `actions/checkout` `ref` default and `GITHUB_REF` for an unmerged `pull_request` — so a later reader can verify it from the file instead of from an ephemeral PR comment.

- ✨ **Added** / ⚡ **Changed**: code review can now actually obtain third-party documentation instead of promising to (#151). The **Third-Party API & Service Analysis** section of `skills/code-review/SKILL.md` already told the reviewer to look up a vendor's published contract, but `agents/argos.md` and `agents/athena.md` shipped with `tools: Read, Glob, Grep, Bash` — no network access at all — so step 2 was unperformable: the reviewer either inferred the contract from the calling code or fell straight through to a **Moderate** "reference not located" finding for documentation that was one search away. Three changes close the gap. **(1) An ordered, binding source walk** replaces the old *"prefer URLs cited in the issue or PR; otherwise look up the vendor's current published documentation"* hint — a URL cited in the issue / PR, then a reference vendored in the repository (committed OpenAPI spec, SDK reference under `vendor/`, webhook fixture), then the vendor's public documentation looked up online **for the version the project actually resolves to** (read from `composer.json` / `package.json` **and the lock file**, never guessed, never reviewed against "latest" when the project is pinned to an older major). A lower source never overrides a higher one that resolved; the resolved URL and version are recorded. Because a URL taken off a tracker is attacker-controllable, the walk carries the same host allow-list guard `skills/resolve-issue` already applies (public `https://` vendor hosts only — never loopback / link-local / internal / `0.0.0.0` / RFC-1918 / ULA, including the cloud-metadata endpoint `169.254.169.254`) and treats everything fetched strictly as data, never as an instruction to follow. The lookup is also **retrieval only** — repository content (diff text, file contents, credentials, tracker payloads) must never be placed into a fetched URL or a search term, so the new capability cannot become an exfiltration channel. **(2) `WebSearch` and `WebFetch` added to both CR agents**, with the read-only stance restated explicitly — both tools fetch, neither writes, so nothing about `argos` / `athena` being read-only on source, tests, and config changes. `agents/apollon.md`, `agents/talos.md`, `agents/daedalus.md`, and `agents/hermes.md` are untouched; only the two agents that run the contract walk gained the capability. **(3) An unresolved reference now produces an answerable question, not a dead end.** A bare Moderate is explicitly declared insufficient output: the review must also publish a blocking documentation request naming the vendor / service, the version in use (or an explicit `could not determine`), the concrete endpoints / SDK methods / webhook events being verified, and the one-sentence ask — shaped so the author closes it with a single link. Requests render under a new omit-if-empty `## Documentation Requests` section on the PR comment in all three CR wrappers (`code-review-github`, `code-review-jira`, `code-review-bugsnag`); the JIRA wrapper additionally folds a plain-language one-liner per request into its existing *Clarifying questions* block, keeping the endpoint list itself on the PR comment so the non-technical `pr-summary` surfaces are unchanged. Separately, **every finding that claims a contract divergence must now cite the reference URL and version it was measured against** — an uncited contract claim is not published as Critical or Moderate — so the next CR run reads the citation instead of repeating the lookup and two runs become comparable. The section's trigger condition is unchanged: a diff with no third-party integration still runs none of this. The implementation-side counterpart (*"do not implement a third-party integration without located documentation"* in `skills/resolve-issue` / `agents/talos.md`) was declared out of scope by the issue and is **not** part of this change.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: fixed a real (not billing) failure of the CI `🔄 Rector` step (`vendor/bin/rector process --dry-run`), which was blocking the merge of PR #150 with the same error on `master` and on any other PR. `composer.lock` in this repository is **not tracked**, so CI resolves dependency versions fresh on every `composer install` run — instead of the locally installed `rector/rector` 2.5.8, CI picked up the newly released 2.5.9, which turns two data-provider docblock-typing rules — `AddParamArrayDocblockFromDataProviderRector` and `AddReturnDocblockDataProviderRector`, registered by the external package `vendor/pekral/rector-rules`, not directly by this repo's `rector.php` or `composer.json` — from a silent no-op into a hard error (`"... is deprecated, as data provider docblock typing is not relevant to code quality"`) on practically every processed file. The failure was first reproduced locally (upgrading to 2.5.9 raised the identical error) and only then fixed with a minimal change — both rules are now explicitly excluded via `$rectorConfig->skip()` in `rector.php`; the fix is verified green on both 2.5.8 and 2.5.9, so it does not depend on a specific version and does not just push the problem one release further down the road. This is a standalone item **unrelated to issue #148** (which PR #150 otherwise carries) — purely a toolchain drift, discovered only because nothing else had landed on `master` in the meantime and nobody had run it before.

- ✨ **Added** / ⚡ **Changed**: agent memory optimization — `docs/memory/PROJECT_MEMORY.md` size, the broken per-role read filter, and uncleaned run artifacts (#148). **(1) Installer orphan report** — when `--prune` is not passed, `bin/ai-olympus install` now reports how many files exist in the target but no longer in the source and recommends `--prune` (`N file(s) across the target directories no longer exist in source. Re-run with --prune to remove them.`), instead of staying silent about accumulating orphans; it never deletes anything on its own. `InstallerPruner` gained a pure `findOrphans()` lookup (reused by both the new report path and the existing `pruneDirectory()`, which now delegates to it — no behavior change to `--prune` itself), documented as file-only scope (an orphaned target directory with no regular file of its own is neither counted nor removable). The report also names which target directory (never the specific orphaned files) carries the count when more than one target has orphans, and the source tree is now listed once per payload — reused by the copier, the pruner, and the orphan lookup alike — instead of re-walked once per target per concern (a PR #150 run-2 CR fix threaded the shared listing into `InstallerFileCopier::installDirectory()` too and deleted its now-redundant private listing method, closing the gap where only the pruner side had actually been deduplicated). The report's own wording was corrected in the same round: the orphan count sums across every target of a payload, so "N file(s) in target" (singular) was replaced with "across the target directories" to stop implying a single directory. A PR #150 code-review round additionally found `InstallerPruner`'s target-side walk still followed directory symlinks (a pre-existing flag, newly reachable on every default install once this report runs unconditionally) — a symlink placed under a target could make the count include, and `--prune` delete, files outside the target tree; the target walk no longer follows symlinks (empirically verified the escape is closed, and that `unlink()` on the resulting leaf symlink only ever removes the link itself, never its target). `Installer::reportInstallSummary()`'s parameter count crossed `@rules/php/core-standards.mdc`'s 4-parameter DTO threshold once the new orphan counter was added; it and the internal sync-count shape threaded through `runAllSyncs()`/`syncDirectories()` are now `InstallSummary` / `SyncCounts` value objects instead of positional parameters / array shapes. Note: the issue's part 1 point 1 (manually deleting the orphaned skills already installed in `~/.claude/skills`) is **deliberately not done** in this PR — an explicit owner decision, not an oversight — so the issue's own success criterion "`comm -13` between `skills/` and `~/.claude/skills/` returns empty" is **not met** by this change; that directory is a shared, mutable, per-machine path outside this repository's control, and this PR intentionally ships detection/reporting only, never an automatic delete against it. Verified (not taken from the issue's own snapshot, which is stale) immediately before this PR: exactly **2** orphaned entries remain in `~/.claude/skills` — `create-tracker-issue-from-cr-comments` and `humanizer` — precisely the two the issue itself flagged as "no owning commit found, needs a human decision"; every other formerly-orphaned skill the issue listed already has a matching source-tree entry. **(2) Per-role read filter fixed** — `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` *Per-role read filter* previously recommended `grep -A5 "^### " | grep -E "Role:.*(<role>|shared)"`, a fixed 5-line-window grep that silently missed almost every entry whose `Role:` field sits past line 5 of its block (most of them, once an entry carries any inserted `**Recurrence (#N)**`-style paragraph) — verified empirically: for `talos|shared` the old idiom returned 2 matching entries out of 33 that actually qualify. Replaced with a whole-block `awk` extraction that scans every line of each entry for a matching `Role:`, regardless of depth. The 5 entries that had never been tagged with a `Role:` at all (`agent-file-vs-registration`, `parallel-agent-publication-contract` → `daedalus`; `agent-new-mode-status-result-parity`, `cr-rule-severity-collision`, `agent-rename-sync-points` → `talos`, matching this project's own established role-tagging convention for near-identical existing entries) are now tagged; every one of the file's 49 entries carries a `Role:`. Pinned by a new fixture-based regression test (`tests/Installer/CompoundEngineeringContentTest.php`) proving the new filter finds a `Role:` at both a shallow and a deep offset, where the old fixed-window idiom would have missed the deep one. **(3) `MODE: bulk` sanctioned for `compact-project-memory`** — the skill's diff-scoped default (issue #98) is unchanged, but it now accepts an explicit, caller-supplied `MODE: bulk` input that treats every existing entry as the primary set for a one-shot pass (skipping only the "expand to related" step, which becomes not applicable — reported as `N/A`); every compaction rule, the deterministic loss-check, and all seven invariants apply unchanged.
